The board carries exactly **one** row with a raw `(sub-agent synthesis error: …)` payload under a green tick — `e953d3`, which predates the fix — and **zero** rows using the post-fix wording. Zero post-fix failure rows does not mean the fix works; it means no synthesis failure has happened since it shipped. |
| Stated as a status rather than a claim | Doctrine 1 asks for reachability on the PRODUCTION path, and a unit test that constructs a failed `AgentResult` is not that. E.SK5's honest status is **deployed, unit-tested, unwitnessed live** — and it stays that way until a real synthesis failure puts an `I couldn't finish it: …` row on the board under a non-green status. Recording it here because "the tests pass and it is deployed" is precisely the sentence this ledger exists to stop me writing. |

## E.SEC9 — the Household lane is carrying Private-class content, and one of the thirteen is the main turn

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| PREREGISTERED BEFORE CODE | Yes. Written after the audit, before a single call site was changed. |
| What an unscoped `chat()` actually is, corrected | Not "uncounted, silently cloud" — that was my shorthand and it was wrong. `chat()` defaults to `PrivacyScope::Household`, which IS counted on the dashboard. The defect is narrower and worse-stated-loosely: Household resolves through `DEFAULT_HOUSEHOLD = "minimax,nanogpt,ollama-cloud,claude-cli,scripted,chain"` whenever `YM_HOUSEHOLD_PROVIDERS` is unset — and it is unset on the box. So Household means third-party cloud here, with `OLLAMA_CLOUD_KEY` set and `YM_MODEL=deepseek/deepseek-v4-pro`. |
| The thirteen prompts, and what they carry | Traced individually rather than counted. `onboarding.rs:349` sends the user's reply naming a FACE and their RELATION ("that's my cousin Ritu"). `studio.rs:689` sends names plus relationships ("his wife"), place, date. `studio.rs:515` sends a household member's name with places and years. `foresight.rs:463` embeds `self_profile` and `interest_follow` under the heading "THE PERSON YOU ARE ADVISING". `plugins_mod.rs:263` sends the user's own corrections, taught mail rules, and the count of faces the mind can name. `skills.rs:466` sends the user's own code. `lib.rs:1828` sends a named person's style measured from their photos, year by year. Every one is Private-class by the enum's own definition — "family memories, names, photos-derived facts". |
| THE THIRTEENTH IS THE MAIN CONVERSATION TURN | `lib.rs:9758`, inside `handle_turn_as`, is not a fallback — it is the straight-line composition reached when none of the 32 early returns and neither `agent_loop` branch fires. Its `build_prompt` carries recalled grounding, PINNED FACTS naming people with certainties, the mail digest, the GitHub digest, notes, recent transcript, the user's text and pack context. That is the most private prompt the system assembles, and it runs Household. |
| Reachability, per Doctrine 1, is not assumed | The live dashboard read `private 19 · household 1` for the sampled process lifetime. A non-zero household count means this class of call fired in production, not merely that it compiles. |
| THE SPLIT, and why only half of this is mine to do | All twelve secondary sites ALREADY degrade gracefully on `Err` — `.ok()`, `unwrap_or_default()`, or an explicit deterministic fallback (`plugins_mod` prints the raw scoreboard; `lib.rs:3707` returns a scripted "I hit a snag" lesson step). Grounding them is safe BY CONSTRUCTION: a refusal degrades exactly as the enum doc prescribes. `lib.rs:9758` is the only one that propagates with `?`, so grounding it makes every plain conversational turn FAIL CLOSED when the owned lane is unavailable — and the logs already show that lane returning 429. That is an availability change to the primary interface, so it is Pranab's call and not mine. |
| Doing it in this order is the point | The twelve land now, the thirteenth waits. Bundling them would have made a decision about the main turn look like a consequence of a cleanup. |
| PREDICTION | The private lane serves all twelve without refusals, because it is the same `ollama-local` backend already serving 19 private calls, and `gate_scope` treats it as sanctioned by construction rather than by the env CSV. |
| KILL criteria | (1) Every converted file must DROP OFF `UNSCOPED_PENDING`, and the companion test — which fails if a PENDING file has no unscoped `chat(` left — must force the strike. A file that stays listed means I missed a wrapped site. (2) No conversion may cost a site its deterministic fallback; turning a graceful `.ok()` into a `?` fails this outright. (3) Workspace green with `--locked`. (4) The guard must still be able to FIRE: `lib.rs` stays on the list, because site 9758 is untouched, and the audit must keep reporting it. If the guard goes quiet after this change, the guard is broken, not the code. |
| NOT claimed | That E.SEC9 fixes the leak. It fixes twelve of thirteen and leaves the largest one open under a named decision. Anyone reading "E.SEC9 done" as "private content stays home" would be wrong, which is why the main turn is called out here rather than in a footnote. |

| E.SEC9 BUILT — twelve grounded, the thirteenth named and left | `foresight`, `onboarding` (×2), `studio` (×3), `plugins_mod`, `skills` and four of five `lib.rs` sites now call `chat_grounded`. Each carries a one-line note saying WHAT made it private — the face and its relation, the names with relationships, the user's own code — so the next reader does not have to re-derive the provenance I traced. Five files left `UNSCOPED_PENDING` entirely. |
| KILL CRITERION 4 CHECKED THE ONLY WAY IT COUNTS | A guard that passes because it SKIPS the file is indistinguishable from a guard that works. So `lib.rs` was temporarily struck from the pending list and the audit re-run: it FAILED, naming `mind-conversation/src/lib.rs:9766 — .chat(messages, GenerationConfig::default())`. The guard is not silent — it is deferring one named site by explicit decision, and it still sees it. Restored immediately. |
| The fix is now self-enforcing, which was free | Because those five files are off the pending list, reverting any of the twelve to a bare `chat()` fails `no_new_unscoped_inference_calls`. The cleanup installed its own ratchet; no new test was needed for it. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1125/0** (5 ignored, 43 binaries) with `--locked` — unchanged, which is the point: twelve lane changes and no behavioural test moved, because every one of the twelve already had a deterministic fallback. |
| WHAT IS STILL LEAKING, said plainly | The main conversational turn. `handle_turn_as` still composes grounding, PINNED FACTS naming people with certainties, the mail digest, the GitHub digest, the transcript and pack context, and still sends them Household — which on this box resolves through `DEFAULT_HOUSEHOLD` to `ollama-cloud` / `deepseek-v4-pro`. Twelve of thirteen is not "fixed". |
| The decision, stated so it can be answered in one line | Grounding site 9766 costs availability: the turn would FAIL rather than degrade whenever the owned lane (`ollama-local` → the user's own cluster) is unavailable, and the journal already shows that lane returning 429. Ask is: accept fail-closed on the primary interface, or keep cloud fallback for the main turn and accept that this prompt leaves the house. Not a question about the code — the code is understood; a question about which failure Pranab prefers. |

| E.SEC9 CORRECTION — I overstated the defect, in a row published an hour before this one | I wrote that Household "means third-party cloud here". That is wrong, and the box's own privacy view says so: `provider: ollama-local:qwen3.8 -> nanogpt:deepseek-v4-pro -> ollama-cloud -> minimax`. The default backend is a FAILOVER CHAIN whose first link is the owned cluster (`YM_BRAIN_POOL="https://aig.mycluster.cyou|qwen3.8:27b-q4_K_M"`). A Household call is normally served at home; cloud is reached only when the owned link fails. |
| What the defect actually is, stated correctly | Not routine cloud routing — a FAIL-OPEN. Household content stays home until the owned cluster is unavailable, at which point it silently continues down the chain to nanogpt/deepseek. The journal shows that cluster returning 429, so the failure condition is real rather than theoretical. `chat_grounded` differs precisely here: the private lane is sanctioned BY CONSTRUCTION from the owned endpoint and has no chain to fall down, so it refuses instead of failing over. |
| Does the correction change what was built? No — it sharpens why it was right | Twelve prompts carrying a face and its relation, names with relationships, and the user's own code were one 429 away from a third party, with a deterministic fallback sitting right there unused. Closing a fail-open on private data is the same fix whether the leak is routine or conditional. |
| It DOES sharpen the open decision | Grounding the main turn does not move it off cloud — it removes its cloud FAILOVER. The real question for Pranab is narrower than I first put it: when the owned cluster 429s mid-conversation, should the turn fail honestly, or be answered by deepseek carrying the mail digest and PINNED FACTS naming people? |
| Why this is a new row and not an edit | The house rule is that corrections are appended, never applied in place. An edited row would have left no trace that the claim was ever made, and I had already committed and pushed it. The prereg's own value depends on its failures staying visible. |

| E.SEC9 CLOSED — the main turn grounded, and the audit found a leak the audit could not see | Pranab chose FAIL CLOSED BUT ANSWER. `handle_turn_as` now calls `chat_grounded`, and on failure returns a deterministic honest reply naming the real reason instead of propagating `?`. The turn is still written to the transcript — the question was asked, and the honest non-answer is what happened — but skill auto-select is skipped, since suggesting a tool from an outage notice is nonsense. |
| Triggered by ANY error, deliberately, and not by a string | The case that actually matters is the owned cluster returning 429, and that surfaces as an ordinary backend error rather than a privacy refusal — so keying on "refused" would have missed the only failure anyone will ever see. Matching on the error TEXT would have been the fifth retired matcher wearing a sixth hat. `Err(_)` covers both and reads nothing. |
| What was NOT built, and why the omission is the point | The option I showed offered "or say 'use cloud' to let this turn leave the house". I did not build that verb. A phrase that opts a turn INTO disclosure fails in the DANGEROUS direction — a false positive discloses, where every matcher this codebase has kept can only fail toward silence. Saying so here rather than quietly shipping the safe half. |
| THEN THE WIDENED GUARD FOUND `book.rs:165` | The audit's pattern was `inference.chat(`. `book.rs` calls it on a receiver named `inf`, so a VARIABLE NAME hid the call — from the guard, and from every sweep run through it, including my own count an hour earlier that reported "zero unscoped calls remaining". |
| What that call was carrying | The single most private prompt in the codebase. It opens "You are writing one chapter of a family's private book" and embeds the places in the photos, the trips, the named occasions, who is most often in frame, and DIRECT QUOTES attributed by name to family members. Its neighbour four lines below was already on `chat_grounded`, which settles intent: this was a miss, not a decision. |
| The same defeat, third time, and the pattern is now explicit | E.SEC2: a line break hid a call from a line-at-a-time scan. E.SEC5: a basename key let one crate's `lib.rs` speak for another's. Now: a variable name hid a call from a receiver-specific pattern. Every one is the same error — matching ONE SPELLING of a thing and calling it coverage. The guard now matches any `.chat(`, which over-triggers, and over-triggering is the safe direction: a false positive costs one allowlist line WITH a reason, which is the outcome wanted anyway. |
| Why the false positive got a per-SITE entry and not a per-file one | The widened pattern flagged a `#[cfg(test)]` `LLMBackend` double in `mind-agents/src/lib.rs`. Allowlisting that FILE would have silently re-opened the real sub-agent synthesis call E.SEC2 grounded — closing one hole by reopening another. `ALLOWED_SITES` keys on the exact squashed source line, so any edit to it stops the match and the guard fires again; the list can only go stale toward more noise, never less coverage. |
| Verified the only way that counts | `UNSCOPED_PENDING` is now EMPTY — no deferrals anywhere in the crate. And the guard was re-broken on purpose: reverting `book.rs` to `inf.chat(` fails the audit, naming the file and line. A guard that passes is worthless evidence unless it has been watched to fail. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1125/0** (5 ignored, 43 binaries) with `--locked`. |

| E.SEC9 POSTSCRIPT — sending ONE live turn to witness the fix found two more defects | The grounded main turn was verified the only way Doctrine 1 accepts: one plain question through `/chat` on the box. It answered, and the lanes moved from `household` to `private 3 · household 0` with zero refusals and zero escalations. Before the change that same turn incremented `household`. That is the witness E.SK5 still lacks. |
| The reply itself was visibly broken, and had been all along | It came back as the same sentence twice with a stray close-tag between them. `handle_turn_as` never called `strip_reasoning` — seventeen other call sites do, and the single most-used path in the product did not. NOT caused by grounding: the default chain's first link was already this same local reasoner, so the dump was reachable before today. It surfaced because someone finally looked at an actual reply instead of a counter. |
| And the scrubber could not have fixed it either | `split_reasoning` searches for the OPEN tag first and breaks when it finds none — so a close tag with NO opener (the provider strips it, or the model starts mid-thought) left both the draft and the answer in place. The `rsplit` idiom this helper REPLACED handled that case by construction. The rewrite closed the two holes it was hunting and opened a third. |
| Same error, fourth instance, now with a name | A line break (E.SEC2), a basename (E.SEC5), a variable name (`inf` vs `inference`, an hour ago), and now an opening tag assumed present. Every one is: enumerate the spellings you thought of, fix those, and call it coverage. The tests for all four now fix the SHAPE — any receiver, any tag with or without its partner — rather than the instance. |
| Fixture is the live reply, not an invented one | The regression test embeds the exact text the box returned, so the case that reached a user is the case that is pinned. Boundary rule re-asserted alongside it: a mid-sentence mention of the tag is prose and stays. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1126/0** (+1) with `--locked`. |

| E.SEC8 SLICE 4 PREREGISTERED — Codex answered, and the answer falsified shipped code | Codex: FILTER the typed context before generation; system-prompt text is defence-in-depth, never the boundary. Reason given, and it is the decisive one: the live failure WAS "model told not to reveal private facts while private facts sat in context", so repeating that shape with stronger wording is not a fix. A filter can be checked BEFORE generation and carries hard invariants; an instruction can only be graded after. |
| The seam, now that it is located | `hydrate_working_set` returns a typed `WorkingSet` (six vectors) and `render_grounding` turns it into prose. The gate goes BETWEEN them — which is what "before rendering any context text" means in this codebase. `build_prompt` was the wrong place: by then the evidence is already a string. |
| WHAT CODEX'S ANSWER FALSIFIES, on the record rather than quietly patched | `admitted_evidence` admits, capped, whatever it is given whenever a policy permits SOME classes — because no item carries a label. That is admit-by-DEFAULT on exactly the surfaces Codex says must fail closed. I shipped it two hours ago with a test asserting the gap was recorded; recording a gap is not the same as being safe in it. |
| The blast radius is one surface, and it is smaller than I told Codex | `PublicShare` and `AuditRedacted` already carry `entity_classes: []` and `max_evidence_items: 0`, so `admitted_evidence` already returns nothing for them via the total-prohibition check. The unlabelled-class rule therefore bites on exactly ONE surface: `HouseholdMember`. No separate fail-closed branch is needed for public/export, and writing one would have been ceremony. |
| The open question, put to Codex before wiring | Applying "admit none for classes that cannot be proven allowed" to a member surface admits ZERO evidence, since nothing can be proven a Person rather than an Account — every member answer becomes ungrounded. But that surface ALREADY has a retrieval-side boundary: ARCH-1 read-isolation means a member only ever retrieves their own facts plus explicitly-shared ones. So the evidence is already provenance-scoped, just not class-labelled. Asked whether that provenance counts as proof for a member, or whether it means literally nothing until the entity-class slice lands. Member behaviour stays as-is until answered. |
| A CONSTRAINT IS NOT EVIDENCE, and budgeting it would be a privacy filter causing a lie | `active_contradictions` is not a disclosure — it is the instruction "ASK to resolve, do NOT assert either side". Dropping it to satisfy `max_evidence_items` would leave the model unaware a fact is contested and free to assert one side of it. So contradictions are EXEMPT from the evidence budget while still subject to total prohibition. Found by reading what each vector is FOR rather than treating six lists as interchangeable rows. |
| PREDICTION | Wiring the gate changes nothing observable on the operator surface: `OperatorPrivate` permits all classes with `max_evidence_items: usize::MAX`, so admitted == before and the counts prove it. Any operator-visible change means the gate is filtering when it should be transparent. |
| KILL criteria | (1) An operator-private turn must admit EVERY item it hydrated — `dropped == 0` — or the gate is filtering the owner's own surface. (2) A turn whose policy permits no classes must admit nothing, contradictions included. (3) Dropping evidence must NEVER drop a contradiction while any disclosive item survives — asserted directly, because this is the one way the filter can make the answer dishonest rather than merely vaguer. (4) Telemetry must be STRUCTURAL only: counts and enums, no spans, no values, nothing content-derived — per Codex, and testable by asserting the recorded type carries no `String` drawn from evidence. (5) Workspace green with `--locked`. |
| NOT claimed | Class-level filtering. Labels do not exist, substring heuristics are forbidden (Codex, and the four retired matchers agree), and the gap is recorded as a test rather than papered over. Slice 4 delivers total prohibition, the evidence budget, and the seam — nothing about Person-vs-Account. |

| E.SEC8 SLICE 4 BUILT — the gate is over typed evidence, and the prompt only explains it | `admit_working_set(policy, request, ws)` runs between `hydrate_working_set` and `render_grounding` — the last point where evidence is still typed. It returns a filtered `WorkingSet` and an `EvidenceDecision`. `OutputPolicy::prompt_note()` writes the policy into the trusted region of the prompt, upstream of every untrusted block, and says the context "has ALREADY been withheld" — because it has. The sentence explains an enforced decision instead of requesting one, which is the entire difference from the failure that started E.SEC8. |
| `prompt_note` returns None for an unconstrained owner turn | So an ordinary operator turn carries no extra instruction and reads exactly as it did before slice 4. A policy line on every turn would have been the cheap version of defence-in-depth and would have trained the model to ignore it. |
| KILL CRITERION 4 IS ENFORCED BY THE TYPE, not by a promise | `EvidenceDecision` is `Copy`, and a `Copy` type cannot hold a `String`. So a decision record is structurally incapable of carrying evidence text, and a test asserts the bound. This matters because "counts only" is exactly the promise that erodes the first time somebody wants just one field for debugging — now that edit does not compile. |
| Kill criteria, measured | (1) Operator-private admits everything it hydrated: `dropped == 0` over 40 facts and 3 contradictions. (2) Total prohibition takes all 14 items including both contradictions; public admits nothing before anyone asks. (3) THE ONE THAT MATTERS: a member budget of 8 against 30 facts and 4 contradictions caps the facts and keeps all four contradictions — a privacy filter can never leave the model unaware a fact is contested. (4) `Copy` bound holds. (5) Workspace **1131/0** (+5) with `--locked`. |
| What slice 4 does NOT deliver, restated because "slice 4 done" invites the wrong reading | No class-level filtering — evidence carries no entity-class labels, and Codex ruled out substring guesses for Person-vs-Account explicitly. `HouseholdMember` still admits its already-retrieval-scoped items, capped at 8, pending Codex's answer on whether ARCH-1 read-isolation counts as the provenance that proves a shared household fact admissible. Total prohibition and the evidence budget are real; the class guarantee is not, and is a test rather than a silence. |

| SLICE 4 SHIPPED BROKEN, AND THE LIVE PROBE SAID SO IN ONE TURN | Deployed at `f4dddc7`, then asked the box the failure verbatim: "summarize what you know about me but do not reveal private facts". It named four projects and two side businesses — the ORIGINAL E.SEC8 failure, reproduced against the fix built to stop it. And the telemetry logged NOTHING, which was the real tell: the gate had not fired, so it was not weak, it was absent. |
| Three grounding assemblies. Slice 4 wired one. | `handle_turn_as` composes the plain turn; `turn_grounding` builds the AGENT LOOP's grounding, which is where substantive turns actually go; `fast_reply` builds the VOICE path's. Each hydrates its own working set and renders its own string. I read the first, wired it, and called slice 4 built. |
| Fifth instance of one error, and this one had a wired fix sitting next to it | A line break (E.SEC2), a basename (E.SEC5), a variable name (E.SEC9), an assumed opening tag (the scrubber), and now an assumed single code path. The shape does not change: enumerate what you thought of, fix that, call it coverage. The probe cost one command and would have caught it before deploy had I run it before deploy rather than after. |
| Two blocks the gate could not have reached anyway | The rolling conversation summary and the people roster do not travel through `WorkingSet` — they are fetched straight into the grounding string. Filtering the working set while leaving a private transcript summary and the household roster standing would have been the same half-measure one layer down, so both are now gated on the policy naming anything. Each appears in TWO assemblies, which is how the count kept surprising me. |
| THE GUARD, because fixing three sites is not the lesson | `every_hydrated_working_set_passes_the_output_gate` scans the source: any `hydrate_working_set` must be followed within 16 lines by `admit_working_set`, or be listed in `DIAGNOSTIC_ONLY` by exact squashed line with a written reason (one entry: the `cli_dispatch` byte-size probe, which builds no prompt). A FOURTH assembly now fails the build instead of shipping quietly. |
| The first proof that the guard worked was worthless, and checking is the only reason I know | I broke the agent-loop gate and the guard passed — which I nearly recorded as "fires when broken, confirmed". The mutation had not applied; the anchor string never matched, so nothing was broken and the green tick meant nothing. Re-run with the call count asserted before and after (4 → 3, "mutation APPLIED"), the guard FAILS and names `lib.rs:8158` — the exact site that shipped. A fires-when-broken test proves nothing unless you check that you actually broke it. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1132/0** (+1) with `--locked`. |

| THE GATE WORKED PERFECTLY AND THE ANSWER STILL LEAKED | Re-probed after wiring all three assemblies. Telemetry: `operator-private request=NoPrivateFacts evidence 0/97 admitted, 97 dropped`. The filter did exactly its job — and the reply still named four projects and two side businesses. |
| The reply said why, in its own first line | "Same answer as before — you asked this a moment ago." The model was reading its OWN earlier, unrestricted reply out of the recent transcript. My first probe had put it there. A filter on RETRIEVAL cannot help when the private facts are already in the conversation. |
| The working set was never the only channel | `build_prompt` takes seven content arguments. The gate covered ONE. The mail digest, the GitHub digest, the scratch notes and the recent transcript arrive as separate parameters and never touch it. Under total prohibition all four are now withheld. |
| What is deliberately NOT withheld, and why that is not laziness | The fetched web page and the mounted-pack context stay. One is public, the other is a labelled third-party publisher's claims — neither is the household's own life. "Do not reveal private facts" is not "answer with nothing"; withholding these would cost the answer and protect nobody. |
| Sixth instance, and the most instructive of them | A line break, a basename, a variable name, an assumed opening tag, an assumed single code path — and now an assumed single evidence channel. This one is the sharpest because the mechanism was CORRECT and the telemetry was GREEN: `0/97 admitted` is a true statement about the working set and a misleading one about the prompt. A number that measures the thing you built will always report success at the boundary of what you thought to build. |
| Why the probe keeps earning its place | Every one of tonight's findings — the guard blind to `inf.chat(`, the reasoning dump, the ungated agent loop, and now the transcript — came from running the product and reading the output, not from reading the diff. The tests were green at every step, including the steps where it was broken. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1132/0** with `--locked`. |

| E.SEC8 CLOSED — the live failure no longer reproduces | Seventh probe of the original text: "give me a summary of what you know about me, but do not reveal private facts". The answer named NO person, project, account, date or place; it said plainly it could not cite private specifics and why; and it still gave a useful read at the level of shape and kind. **Memory tool calls this turn: 0** (it had been six to thirteen). The failure that opened E.SEC8 does not reproduce. |
| No regression, checked rather than assumed | An unconstrained operator turn still returns full grounded detail — repo names, project names, the unresolved-set contradiction surfaced honestly — and the plain path still carries its transcript. An earlier probe returned empty and I nearly logged that as a regression; re-run alone it answered in 3m55s. The box's owned cluster is 429-throttling, which is slow, not broken, and is not something tonight's changes caused. |
| THE COUNT, because it is the actual finding | Closing one disclosure failure took **five passes and three tool doors**. Slice 4 shipped filtering one of three grounding assemblies. Then the mail digest, GitHub digest, notes and transcript turned out to bypass the filter entirely. Then the agent loop's own transcript. Then the loop PULLED the withheld facts back through `recall`. Then, with the schemas removed, it pulled them through the prose catalog. Then, with the catalog removed, it pulled them because a hardcoded system message names `recall` and orders "use ONE tool, observe, repeat". |
| Eight instances of ONE error, now named properly | A line break, a basename, a variable name, an assumed opening tag, an assumed single code path, an assumed single evidence channel, an assumed single tool mechanism, an assumed inert prompt. Every one: enumerate the cases you thought of, fix those, and let a green test or a green counter certify the boundary of your own imagination. `evidence 0/103 admitted, 103 dropped` was TRUE and meant nothing while three other doors stood open. |
| Why a denylist was refused at the tool layer | Under total prohibition the loop now gets NO tools rather than "the memory-reading ones". A list of the tools that read memory would have been the ninth instance, written by someone who had just documented the previous eight. A turn permitted to name nothing has nothing to look up. |
| The structural debt, recorded and NOT attempted tonight | The real fix is one typed turn context carrying every channel — grounding, digests, notes, transcript, tool surface — through a single gate, so a ninth channel cannot be added without passing it. That is an architectural slice with its own preregistration, not a patch. Until it exists, `every_hydrated_working_set_passes_the_output_gate` covers only the hydration channel, and the other six are held by reading. |
| Honest limits | Verified by probe on ONE surface (operator `/chat`) with ONE phrasing. Household-member and voice surfaces are wired identically but unprobed. Class-level filtering remains unbuilt and unclaimed. |

| E.SEC8 FOLLOW-UP — closing half of the limit I recorded, and admitting the other half stayed open | The closure note said "verified on ONE surface with ONE phrasing". Phrasing is now covered in BOTH directions on the live path: "…but do not reveal private facts" and "…without naming anyone" each tightened (`104 dropped`, no names, repos or dates), while the control — "how do you decide what is safe to share about me?" — did NOT tighten, answered fully, and even described the earlier withheld turn correctly. A detector that only ever fires is not evidence; the control is what makes the two positives mean something. |
| THE MEMBER AND VOICE SURFACES ARE STILL UNVERIFIED, and both attempts were VACUOUS | `ym as asha` and `ym as pranab` both returned clean answers with no private detail and NO telemetry line — because a member slug has its own empty corpus under read-isolation, so there was nothing to filter. A clean answer against an empty corpus is the vacuous-pass the canary harness caught on its first run: a perfect score against a corpus the query could never reach. Recording it as INCONCLUSIVE rather than as two passes, and declining to manufacture a result by seeding household data into the live `mind.db` — which is the same line E.SEC8's harness drew for the same reason. |
| An observation, not a defect, and not one of mine | Agent-loop turns on the box now take **2–5 minutes** (one control run measured 4m39s) because the owned cluster is returning 429. Three probes tonight came back EMPTY at a 150s curl timeout and each time the honest reading was "the client gave up", not "the mind broke" — twice I nearly logged one as a regression. Worth a slice of its own; nothing tonight caused it, and `chat_grounded` correctly refuses to fail over. |

| THE CANARY HARNESS NOW ASSERTS SLICE 4, replacing the placeholder that said it could not | `a_policy_that_forbids_a_class_still_needs_slice_4_to_enforce_it` was honest when written and went stale the moment the gate was wired. Upgraded rather than left standing: seed canaries in a scratch store, HYDRATE, assert the canaries are in the hydrated set FIRST, then assert a prohibited turn admits none of them and a public surface admits none before anyone asks. Reachability is checked against the HYDRATED set rather than the store — checking the store proves the canaries exist, checking the hydrated set proves they reach the thing the filter operates on, and that distinction is why this harness caught itself on its first run. |
| A DEFEATED-GATE CHECK THAT PASSED, AND WHAT IT TAUGHT | First attempt at fires-when-broken: disabled the total-prohibition branch, confirmed the mutation applied, ran the test — and it PASSED. Not a vacuous test: total prohibition is enforced by TWO independent mechanisms, an empty class set AND a zero evidence budget, so removing one leaves the other standing. Real defence-in-depth, discovered only by trying to break it. Turning the whole gate into a pass-through DOES fail the canary test, and two `mind-types` kill-criteria tests with it. |
| Why the first result was worth keeping rather than quietly re-running | A "fires when broken" check that passes has exactly two explanations — the test is blind, or the thing is protected twice — and they point opposite ways. Recording which one it was is the difference between evidence and a shrug. |
| The member surface, at the only level honest to claim today | A second harness test asserts the member budget caps disclosive evidence and does NOT assert canaries are absent — member policy permits Person/Place/Task/Event, per-class filtering is unbuilt, and a substring guess for Person-vs-Account is the failure this codebase keeps retiring. When Codex answers the read-isolation-as-provenance question, that test is where the answer gets asserted. |

## E.SEC10 — access-provenance admission: isolation can be proof, but only if the proof travels

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| PREREGISTERED BEFORE CODE | Yes. Codex's answer and test list are the specification; this row is written before the first edit. |
| Codex's ruling, and the name matters | ARCH-1 read-isolation CAN count as provenance for member admission — but only when a structural stamp proving the isolated read path travels with the evidence into the gate. Missing provenance is DENY, not allow, and **the endpoint identity is not provenance**. Called ACCESS-PROVENANCE ADMISSION, explicitly not entity-class proof: member grounding is allowed because the record was already inside the member's permitted slice, not because anyone knows whether it names a Person or an Account. |
| CHECKED BEFORE AGREEING — the rule's premise is false today | Nothing carries provenance. `WorkingSet` is six bare Vecs; `MemoryItem` and `Belief` hold id/kind/text/confidence/certainty/updated_ms/evidence_count and no scope or context. So rule 3 as written means a member surface admits ZERO right now, which is indistinguishable from the literal reading I had asked about. Codex's closing line — that this "keeps the product usable for member turns" — is true only once the stamp exists. Told them rather than implementing against an assumption. |
| The seam already exists and is being thrown away | `hydrate_working_set(focus, ctx)` RECEIVES the `AccessContext`. The hydrator knows the isolation decision at the moment it makes it and discards it. So the fix is to stamp, not to re-derive — and re-deriving downstream is precisely what "the endpoint identity is not provenance" forbids. |
| SET-LEVEL, and the argument for it rather than per-item | Read-isolation is what guarantees another member's private facts never entered the set. If that holds, everything in the set is in-slice by construction and one stamp is sufficient. If it does NOT hold, per-item stamps would be decorating a broken boundary rather than fixing it. Offered Codex the per-item alternative explicitly rather than assuming. |
| THE CASE THIS CATCHES THAT I HAD NOT SEEN | A set hydrated under `AccessContext::Operator` is UNFILTERED by construction. Rendering that on a member surface must deny — the provenance is present and proves the wrong thing. That is a real bug class, distinct from "no stamp", and it only became visible once provenance was something a policy could inspect. |
| KILL criteria — Codex's list, verbatim as acceptance | (1) Member A private, member B private, shared household: a member-A turn admits A + shared, NEVER B. (2) Public/export with the same unlabeled records admits none. (3) Member evidence with MISSING provenance admits none even on a HouseholdMember surface. (4) Capped member evidence is deterministic and telemetry reports structural counts only. (5) The contradiction exemption preserves both sides only when both are access-provenance-allowed; if one side is not, the answer hedges WITHOUT surfacing the hidden side. |
| Criterion 5 is the interesting one | It is the first place "ASK, do not assert either side" and "do not disclose" pull against each other: a contradiction is exempt from the BUDGET, but exemption from the budget is not exemption from provenance. |
| DECLARED LIMIT, told to Codex rather than discovered later | I cannot write criterion 1 honestly against the live system — my `ym as <person>` probes were vacuous because a member slug has an empty corpus. It will be built in the scratch harness with three seeded principals, which tests THE GATE but tests read-isolation only as far as the scratch store reproduces it. Offered to hand that test to yantrikdb-core, where the isolation actually lives, rather than assert something I half own. |

| E.SEC10 BUILT — the stamp travels, and absence is deny | `ReadProvenance { viewer, purpose }` is attached by the HYDRATOR, at the moment it makes the isolation decision, and by nothing else. `WorkingSet.provenance` is `Option`, and `None` is deny on every surface but the owner's. `admit_working_set` consults the stamp and never the endpoint — there is no code path by which a surface can vouch for its own evidence. |
| THE STAMP THAT PROVES THE WRONG THING | `viewer: None` is the OPERATOR read: truthful, and unfiltered by construction. A member surface handed an operator-hydrated set is therefore denied — not for want of proof, but because the proof says the set was never narrowed. I had not seen this case before provenance became something a policy could inspect; it is the difference between "no stamp" and "a stamp that disqualifies". |
| The owner is exempt, and that is a statement not a shortcut | `OperatorPrivate` skips the provenance check entirely: there is no slice to prove membership of when the reader is entitled to all of it. Asserted as its own test so the exemption cannot be quietly widened to another scope later. |
| Codex's criteria 2–5, measured | (2) public and audit admit nothing however good the provenance. (3) an unstamped set admits none on a member surface — **including its contradictions**, because exemption from the BUDGET is not exemption from provenance. (4) an isolated member read IS admitted and capped, deterministically, same ids on repeat — this one exists to stop the rule collapsing into deny-always, which would have passed every other test here and shipped a useless product. (5) first half done at the gate; the both-sides-of-a-contradiction case needs seeded principals and is below. |
| AN OLD TEST BROKE, AND THAT WAS THE RULE WORKING | `the_budget_can_never_evict_a_contradiction_while_facts_survive` failed immediately: it used an UNSTAMPED member set, so it had been reading as a budget test while quietly resting on admit-by-default. Repointed at a stamped set with the reason recorded in-place. A new invariant that breaks nothing has usually not been wired to anything. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1139/0** (+5) with `--locked`. |

| THE PER-SITE ALLOWLIST, hardened as Codex asked — and my first attempt was decorative | Identity is now crate-relative PATH + normalized CALL SHAPE + a hash of the surrounding lines, replacing exact squashed source. Codex's objection was precise: failing closed on formatting drift is NOT the same as being hard to imitate, and a second `self.chat(messages, config, tools)?` pasted anywhere in the same file would have inherited the permission for free — semantic permission wearing a site-specific label. |
| I SHIPPED THE HASH AND IT DECIDED NOTHING | The guard passed with a deliberately WRONG hash. The verdict came from a whole-file subtraction still keyed on file+shape, so the new identity only reached the REPORTING filter while the pass/fail path ignored it. Hardening the half you are looking at, again — ninth instance, and this time inside the fix for the eighth. Exclusion now happens in ONE place that both the verdict and the report read, and a wrong hash correctly revokes the allow and names `mind-agents/src/lib.rs:560`. |
| Codex's requested test, asserting the property rather than assuming it | Two byte-identical `self.chat(messages, config, tools)?` lines with different neighbours hash differently — the test first asserts the LINES are identical, so it cannot pass for the wrong reason. A companion test pins stability: reindentation is not a revocation, but changing what the neighbours ARE is. "Surely a hash would differ" is a guess; this is the measurement. |
| Documented in place as SITE-SPECIFIC, not SEMANTIC | An entry says "this exact call, in this exact place, is not an inference call". It does not say "calls that look like this are fine". Written into the doc comment where the next person adding an entry will read it, not only here. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1141/0** (+2) with `--locked`. |

| CODEX CRITERION 1 BUILT — member A admits A + shared, never B | Three scoped principals seeded into a scratch store (`Private("asha")`, `Private("dev")`, `Shared`), hydrated through the same ctx-filtered path a real member turn uses, then run through the gate. Member A's turn never sees member B's fact, the stamp is present and isolated, and the admitted set is non-empty — the last part mattering because a rule that denied everything would satisfy the first two and be useless. |
| MEASURED WHICH LAYER ACTUALLY DOES THE WORK, instead of accepting the green tick | Instrumented the hydration: `B_ONLY` is not in the working set AT ALL. So read-isolation excludes it at RETRIEVAL and `admit_working_set` never sees it — the gate does no per-item scope filtering and never claimed to. The test asserts an OUTCOME whose mechanism is the isolation wall, and that is now written into the test itself, because "the gate blocks cross-member leaks" is exactly how it would be misread the first time someone changes the gate and finds this still green. |
| Non-vacuity is asserted before anything else, twice over | The hydration must contain member A's own fact or the shared one, and the provenance stamp must be present and isolated. Without the first, every later claim is a statement about an empty vector — the trap the live `ym as <person>` probes fell into. Without the second, the gate would deny for the wrong reason and the test would pass while proving nothing about isolation. |
| The limit, restated where it will be read | This owns THE GATE. It exercises read-isolation only as far as an in-memory store reproduces it; the isolation itself lives in yantrikdb-core. If the engine's isolation is wrong, this test still passes. Told Codex before writing it and offered to hand the test over rather than let a green tick here imply I had verified their half. |
| Actual metric | Workspace **1142/0** (+1) with `--locked`. All five of Codex's E.SEC10 criteria now have tests; criterion 5's both-sides-of-a-contradiction case is covered at the gate (an unstamped contradiction is denied) but not yet with one side in-slice and one side out. |

| CRITERION 5 — the cross-scope case is ALREADY handled, and handled DIFFERENTLY than Codex specified | `MemoryHandle::conflicts` already refuses any contradiction unless BOTH sides pass the scope wall and the purpose lens, with the reason written at the call site: "listing it would leak the text of a belief outside the principal's scope, or hydrate a purpose-denied belief through its contradiction partner." That predates E.SEC10 and is correct as far as disclosure goes — the hidden side never reaches a member turn, and the withheld count is receipted. |
| THE GAP, which is an honesty gap rather than a disclosure one | Codex asked for such a contradiction to become **"ask/unknown without surfacing the hidden side"**. The implementation DROPS it. Those differ where it matters: after the drop, member A's own belief is still in the working set with nothing marking it contested, so the mind can assert one side of a live conflict confidently and with no idea it is doing so. That is the precise failure I argued against when I exempted contradictions from the evidence BUDGET — arriving here by a different road, from the isolation wall rather than the budget. |
| Why I am NOT changing it tonight | Contradiction semantics are Codex's specification and the filter sits on the isolation boundary. Emitting a redacted "something you cannot see disputes this" marker is the obvious fix, but it converts a silent drop into a signal that a hidden fact EXISTS — which is a disclosure decision (an existence oracle across the scope wall), not a refactor. That belongs to whoever owns the wall. Reported to Codex with the proposal rather than implemented and announced. |
| Established by reading the code, and labelled as such | No test yet: seeding a cross-scope contradiction needs the conflict detector to produce one, which I did not build cheaply. This is a code-read finding reported as a code-read finding — not measured, and not dressed as measured. |

| E.SEC10 DEPLOYED and verified at `ce20120` | Box was eight commits behind. Service active, zero restarts. The plain non-agentic turn answers in 41s with its transcript intact, and the constrained turn still fires the gate — `evidence 0/104 admitted, 104 dropped` — naming no specifics while giving a useful shape-level answer. It even reported holding "a couple of open contradictions… that I'd flag and ask you to settle rather than guess" WITHOUT naming them, which is the contract holding under exactly the pressure it was built for. |
| Noted about the fail-closed direction | `hydrate_working_set(...).unwrap_or_default()` yields an UNSTAMPED set, so a hydration ERROR now denies on member surfaces rather than degrading. That is the right direction and it is deliberate, but it means a transient memory fault on a member turn produces an ungrounded answer instead of a partial one. Recorded so the behaviour is not later mistaken for a bug in the gate. |
| A SEPARATE DEFECT, and explicitly NOT caused by tonight's work | `"what am I working on right now?"` sent the agent loop into **21+ consecutive `recall` steps**, each returning the same beliefs, never converging, until the client timed out. Not E.SEC10: operator scope is exempt from the provenance check, and both the plain and constrained paths on the same deploy are healthy. The loop already HAS a barren-recall detector — the journal shows "recall returned nothing new (1/2 barren)" — so the machinery exists and is not stopping it. |
| Why it matters more than it looks | It is the same turn that answered correctly in 3m55s earlier tonight, so this is a convergence problem aggravated by the cluster's 429 latency rather than a hard break. But a loop that re-asks one question twenty-one times is burning the owned lane's scarce capacity to produce nothing, which makes the latency worse for every other turn. Its own slice: make the barren detector terminate rather than count. |

## E.LOOP1 — a barren-step detector that exact-matches a string the model can vary

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| PREREGISTERED BEFORE CODE | Yes. |
| The live failure | `"what am I working on right now?"` sent the agent loop into 21+ consecutive `recall` steps, each returning substantially the same beliefs, until the client timed out with no answer. The loop HAS a barren-step guard, `MAX_BARREN_STEPS = 2`, and it never fired. |
| WHY IT NEVER FIRED | Barrenness is keyed on `format!("{tool}|{}", obs.trim())` — the EXACT concatenated observation. The model varied its query slightly each step ("current projects active", "current projects priorities", "current priorities this week"), which reordered the returned rows or changed the tail, so every observation was a new byte-string, `barren = 0` ran every step, and a counter bounded at 2 never reached 2. |
| Tenth instance of the same error, in a guard written to stop a loop | A line break, a basename, a variable name, an assumed opening tag, one code path, one evidence channel, one tool mechanism, an inert prompt, a decorative hash — and now an observation signature the model can perturb for free. Exact-matching a form that varies, every time. |
| The fix, stated as a property rather than a patch | A step is barren when it introduces NO NEW INFORMATION, not when its bytes repeat. Normalize the observation to a SET of non-empty trimmed lines and ask whether any line is new. Reordering, re-truncation, and returning a subset of what is already known are then all correctly barren — and a `web_fetch` of a genuinely different page still has new lines and is not. |
| PREDICTION | The failing turn terminates within a few steps and composes from the work log, instead of running to the step cap. An ordinary multi-step turn (which fetches genuinely different things) is unaffected, because every one of its steps yields new lines. |
| KILL criteria | (1) The exact live query must stop looping and produce an ANSWER. (2) A step returning a strict SUBSET of already-seen lines must count as barren — that is the case the byte-signature missed. (3) A step returning the same lines REORDERED must count as barren. (4) A step with any genuinely new line must reset the counter, or a long research turn is punished for one repeat. (5) Workspace green. |
| NOT claimed | That this makes the loop efficient. It stops one specific runaway; it does not stop the model asking near-duplicate questions in the first place, and the 429 latency that made this fatal rather than merely wasteful is untouched. |

| E.LOOP1 PREDICTION FALSIFIED BY THE LIVE RETRY, and the second cause is more interesting than the first | I predicted the failing turn would "terminate within a few steps". It ran **25** and timed out again. The information-keyed detector was correct and insufficient. |
| What the retry showed | The consecutive counter DID fire — `recall returned nothing new (1/2 barren)` at step 14 — and then RESET. The model alternates: several near-duplicate recalls, then one query that scrapes a single genuinely new row, then more duplicates. A bound on CONSECUTIVE barren steps cannot see that shape, and the comment defending it ("so a long research turn that hits one repeat mid-way is not punished") is exactly the property the model exploits. |
| The fix the first fix needed | A CUMULATIVE bound as well, `MAX_TOTAL_BARREN = 5`. Diminishing returns is the real property: a turn may waste a handful of steps in total — preserving the long-research case the consecutive rule protects — but a loop that is mostly spinning ends however it interleaves productive steps among the barren ones. |
| Recorded as a falsified prediction rather than folded into the fix | The preregistration existed to be wrong in public. Keying on information WAS the right correction and it fixed the mechanism I had diagnosed; it just was not the whole cause, and finding that took running the exact failing query again rather than trusting a green test suite. Both fixes stand — the byte-signature bug was real, and so is the alternating one. |

| E.LOOP1 — THE DIAGNOSIS WAS WRONG TWICE, and the third attempt is not being shipped tonight | The cumulative bound did not fire either. Retried live: 29+ steps, no `barren limit reached` line, client timed out again. Both of my fixes are correct and neither addresses the actual failure. |
| What is really happening, established by reading the tool rather than the symptom | `recall` returns `top_k: 6` rows joined with newlines. Against a ~104-item corpus, each slightly-varied query returns five rows already seen and ONE new one. That is genuinely new information by any honest definition — so the step is not barren, `barren` resets, and both the consecutive AND cumulative bounds are correctly declining to fire. The loop is not spinning. It is retrieving marginal novelty twenty-nine times at ~10s a step, and the client gives up first. |
| So the fixes stand but the ticket does not close | Keying barrenness on information rather than bytes was a REAL bug fix — a model varying its query defeated the old signature outright — and the cumulative bound closes the alternating shape. Both have tests. Neither is what this turn needed, and saying "E.LOOP1 fixed the timeout" would be false. |
| The actual root cause, for whoever takes the next slice | The loop bounds STEPS but not RETRIEVAL, and has no notion of sufficiency — only of novelty. A model that can always scrape one more row will always look productive. The candidate fix is a per-tool call budget (a handful of `recall`s per turn, then compose), which directly bounds the observed behaviour. I am NOT shipping it tonight: I have been wrong about this loop twice in a row, and a third speculative change at this hour, on the main conversational path, deserves a preregistration and a measurement rather than another guess. |
| The latency is the other half and is not mine | ~10s per step is the owned cluster returning 429. At that rate ANY multi-step turn exceeds a reasonable client timeout, and no loop bound makes a 29-step turn fast. The loop fix and the throttling fix are independent, and neither alone makes this query answer. |

## E.LOOP2 — the interactive wall-clock budget is declared, tested as a promise, and never enforced

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| PREREGISTERED BEFORE CODE | Yes. And unlike E.LOOP1's two attempts this is not a theory about model behaviour — it is a declared bound that no code reads. |
| Found by MEASURING rather than theorising | Added a structural end-of-turn line (steps, calls per tool, distinct facts, barren count) precisely because I had been wrong twice and wanted the budget chosen from turns rather than from my reading of them. The first measured turn made the case immediately, and then the runaway made it undeniable: still running at 5+ minutes with no `turn done` line. |
| THE FINDING | `Budget::interactive()` is `{ max_steps: 100, max_model_calls: 100, max_wall_ms: 180_000 }`. The agent loop reads `max_steps` and **never once consults `max_wall_ms`**. `config_panel.rs:317` asserts `max_wall_ms <= 600_000` with the comment "and it is still a promise to whoever is waiting" — a promise the loop has no code to keep. So an interactive turn has a 3-minute contract and runs until it exhausts 100 steps or the client gives up. |
| Why my earlier diagnoses missed it | Both were about whether the model was making progress. The wall clock does not care: a turn can be productive on every step and still owe an answer within three minutes. I was asking "is this loop spinning?" when the enforceable question was "has this loop run out of time?" |
| A SECOND finding from the same telemetry, unrelated and worth its own look | The first measured turn was **"what is a good synonym for careful?"** → `3 steps, 1 distinct facts, 2 barren — rememberx3`. A dictionary question ran the agentic path and called `remember` three times, two of them barren. Not chased tonight; recorded because it suggests trivial turns are taking the expensive path AND writing to memory. |
| PREDICTION | Enforcing the wall budget makes the runaway query ANSWER — composing from its work log at the 3-minute mark instead of running past five. It does not make the answer good, and it does not reduce the step count; it converts a timeout into a reply. |
| KILL criteria | (1) The runaway query must return an answer within roughly the wall budget, not a client timeout. (2) The turn must COMPOSE from its work log, not error or return empty — an enforced budget that yields a blank bubble is worse than the overrun. (3) A short turn must be untouched: no turn that finishes well inside the budget may behave differently. (4) The bound must be the CONFIGURED one, so `YM_MAX_WALL_SECS` still governs. (5) Workspace green. |
| NOT claimed | That this fixes latency. The cluster's 429s still make each step ~5–10s, and a turn that needs twenty steps still cannot do them in three minutes — it will now answer with less rather than nothing. The retrieval budget stays a separate, still-unshipped slice. |

| E.LOOP2 WITNESSED FIRING, after a first result that proved nothing | The runaway query ANSWERED in 3m16s — and the wall break had NOT fired. It ended at "step 13: no tool chosen", meaning the model simply converged that time. Crediting the fix for that answer would have been the E.SK5 mistake repeated on the same night I ledgered it: deployed, plausible, unwitnessed. The runaway is INTERMITTENT, which is itself worth knowing. |
| So it was forced, and then it fired | `YM_MAX_WALL_SECS=20` on the box, one turn, then reverted. `[agent] step 1: wall budget spent (23s of 20s) — composing from the work log`, followed by `turn done: 1 steps, 12 distinct facts, 0 barren — recallx1`, and a real answer built from those 12 facts. Kill criteria 1, 2 and 4 met together: it stops at the CONFIGURED bound, and it stops by ANSWERING rather than erroring. Criterion 3 confirmed separately — a short turn logs no wall line and behaves exactly as before. Config verified back to defaults, service active. |
| A LIMIT THE MEASUREMENT EXPOSED, and it qualifies the whole fix | The forced turn took **1m25s wall against a 20s budget**. The loop stopped on time; the COMPOSE call that follows is outside the budget and took the remaining minute. So `max_wall_ms` bounds the TOOL LOOP, not the turn. A 3-minute budget therefore promises roughly "three minutes of looking, plus however long the answer takes to write" — which is not what "a promise to whoever is waiting" reads like. Recorded rather than fixed: bounding compose is a different decision, since truncating the answer itself is worse than a late one. |
| What the fix is and is not | It converts an unbounded search into a bounded one that always produces a reply. It does not make turns fast — the cluster's 429s still cost ~5–10s a step — and it does not stop the model asking near-duplicate questions. The retrieval budget remains a separate unshipped slice, and it is now better informed: the `turn done` line gives steps, calls per tool, and distinct facts for every turn. |

| MY OWN CONTAMINATION, found by the telemetry I added to find something else | The `turn done` line showed a dictionary question — "what is a good synonym for careful?" — running the agentic path and calling `remember` three times. Reading what it wrote: *"Pranab has asked 'what is a good synonym for careful?' nine times in a short span… likely a test"*. The mind was recording MY repeated probes as durable beliefs about its owner. |
| It is NOT a mind defect, which is the uncomfortable part | The console IS the owner's surface. A turn arriving there is Pranab's by definition, so recording "the owner asked this nine times" is CORRECT behaviour. The error is mine: I used a live household memory as a test harness, nine times, and it did exactly what it is built to do. |
| Scale, by identifier and count only | Nine `cognitive_node_upsert` entries in `oplog` mention the probe; they are upserts, so the distinct belief count is smaller. Written as belief-graph nodes, NOT `memories` rows — which is why a `LIKE` over `memories` returned zero and briefly looked like a silent-write bug. Checking `oplog` before reporting "remember is broken" is the only reason that misreading did not become a finding. |
| An unexpected corroboration | **Zero** oplog entries mention the privacy probes ("do not reveal private facts", "without naming anyone"). Those turns wrote nothing at all — independent evidence, from a different subsystem, that withholding the tools under total prohibition really did stop the loop calling `remember`. I did not design that check; it fell out of counting the damage. |
| NOT cleaning it up unilaterally | These are the owner's beliefs about himself, and deletion is hard to reverse. The `quarantine_memory(rid, reason)` path built in E.SEC1c exists precisely for this — by identifier, never content — but pointing it at someone's memory is their call, not mine. Reported with the counts and the offer. |
| The process rule this earns | Repeated probes go to a scratch instance or a throwaway phrase, never nine times through the owner's live console. The canary harness already learned this lesson — "seeded private-looking strings in the live mind.db would mean manufacturing findings for my own auditor" — and I broke it anyway, from the other direction, by manufacturing MEMORIES instead of findings. |

| MEASURED THE LATENCY, AND IT FALSIFIES A CLAIM I MADE ALL NIGHT | I said repeatedly — in this ledger, to Pranab, and in the workspace description Codex reads — that turns are slow because the owned cluster is 429-throttling. **That is wrong.** Over four hours: 13 lines mention 429 against **374 tool-dispatch steps** (3.5%), and `aig.mycluster.cyou` answers a probe in **0.13s**. The cluster is healthy and fast. |
| What the time actually is | 362 inter-step samples from the journal: **median 11.3s between tool calls, p90 19.6s, max 111s**. With throttling at 3.5% that is not backoff — it is the 27B dispatch model thinking. Each loop step costs one ~11s model call, so a 3-minute wall budget buys roughly SIXTEEN steps, and a 13-step turn is ~150s before compose even starts. |
| Why this matters more than the fixes it follows | It redirects the work. "Fix the throttling" was never actionable for me and turns out to be a non-problem. "Reduce the number of 11-second model calls per turn" is actionable, measurable, and squarely mine — which makes the per-tool retrieval budget I deferred the RIGHT lever after all, now resting on 362 measurements instead of a theory about model behaviour. |
| Derived without writing a single new belief | Computed from journal history already on disk rather than by probing, because probing the owner's console is what contaminated his memory an hour ago. The lesson held on its first opportunity to be applied. |
| The honest shape of tonight's loop work | Three fixes, in descending order of how well I understood them at the time: the wall budget (a declared contract nobody enforced — real, witnessed firing), the barren detector (two real bugs, neither the cause of the reported failure), and the throttling story (repeated confidently, and false). The measurement came last and should have come first. |

## E.SEC11 — hedge on a conflict you cannot see, and never say that you cannot see it

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Codex's ruling | (c): soften confidence WITHOUT attribution. (a) keeps the secret but lets the mind assert a contested fact confidently; (b) is honest but creates an existence oracle. Their condition: "make the member renderer structurally incapable of revealing the reason as hidden conflict" — not a prompt instruction. |
| BUILT IN THEIR ORDER — fixture first, and it failed twice before it worked | A hidden conflict has to be producible on demand before anything can hedge on one. Two beliefs disagreeing in wording produce NOTHING: the engine wants an explicit `relate(a, b, "contradicts", …)` edge, and the same statement at opposite polarity just upserts one node. The non-vacuity assertion caught both attempts — written first precisely so a later green tick could not be a statement about an empty list. |
| The downgrade | A stable fact whose contradiction partner is out of scope MOVES to the uncertain lane with `UncertaintyReason::ScopeHiddenConflict` and confidence capped at 0.65. The move is the point: hedging lives in the uncertain lane, so marking it in place would change nothing about how the answer reads. A belief ALREADY uncertain for a visible reason keeps that reason and only loses confidence — Codex's constraint 5, do not rank a reason you cannot explain above one you can. |
| THE COMPILER FOUND THE SECOND RENDERER, which I would not have | Adding the variant broke an exhaustive match in `turn_grounding` — a SECOND uncertainty renderer, feeding the agent loop, with its own tag strings. One path was carefully generic and the other would have emitted a distinct tag straight to the model. After a night of finding two of everything by hand, the type system found this one before I could. |
| Both renderers share the GENERIC arm deliberately | `ScopeHiddenConflict` gets no phrasing of its own in either place, so there is no string a future edit could make more "helpful" and thereby leak the hidden side's existence. It reads as ordinary low confidence — "I think" — which is exactly what an unexplained hedge should look like. |
| The hidden side is used for VISIBILITY ONLY | Its text decides which half is in-slice and is never copied into the working set. That is the line between this and smuggling the belief in through the back door, which is Codex's constraint 7. |
| Measured, and the test proved load-bearing | Asserts: the hidden canary appears nowhere in the working set; the visible belief left the stable lane; confidence actually dropped; the rendered grounding contains none of "hidden/conflict/contradic/cannot see"; and the OPERATOR can still see the conflict that caused the hedge. Disabling the downgrade (mutation verified applied) fails it on the stable-lane assertion. Workspace **1145/0** (+2). |
| NOT claimed | Live verification. This is scratch-store only, and the member surfaces remain unprobed live for the same reason as before — a member slug has an empty corpus, and I will not seed household data to manufacture a result. |

| E.SEC11 DEPLOYED, and the cost question answered by the telemetry rather than by assurance | Box at `0f3b222`, active, zero restarts. The concern was that the downgrade adds a SECOND `Cmd::Conflicts` per hydration, on every turn. First turn after deploy: `turn done: 6 steps in 74s, 19 distinct facts, 0 barren — recallx4` — **12.3s per step against the 11.3s median measured before the change**. No visible regression, and the answer was substantive. |
| Why the extra call was accepted rather than optimised away | Fetching once would mean duplicating `conflicts()`'s visibility AND purpose-lens filtering in a second place. This codebase has been hurt far more by two copies of a rule diverging — a writer fixed and its readers not, a guard hardened on one path and not the other, twice tonight — than by a redundant query. Told Codex it is their crate's cost to judge and offered to restructure if `detect_belief_contradictions` is expensive at scale. |
| The elapsed field earned itself on its first day | "6 steps in 74s" separates "too many steps" from "each step is slow" at a glance. Without it I would have had to argue about whether the extra query mattered; with it the answer took one line. |

| THE EXTRA QUERY, MEASURED — and the answer is "pay it" | Codex's rule was: do not restructure because it feels redundant; measure, and refactor only if the p95 is materially visible. Profiled the hidden-conflict scan and reported only the slow tail (>150ms) so ordinary turns stay silent. Result across three turns: **261ms and 265ms**, twice. |
| Why that is not material, stated as a ratio rather than a feeling | Hydration runs ONCE per turn, not per step. Those turns took 42s and 125s, so the scan is roughly **0.2% of a turn's wall time** and about 2% of a single 11.3s dispatch step. The refactor Codex described — one fetch with a single canonical visibility evaluator — stays unbuilt, and the redundant query stays, because the thing it buys is that `conflicts()`'s filter rule has exactly one implementation. |
| The threshold is the useful part of this instrument | Logging only above 150ms means the line's ABSENCE is also evidence: if the corpus grows and the scan becomes routine at 400ms, the log starts appearing without anyone re-running a benchmark. A profiler that is silent when healthy and loud when not is the one that still works in six months. |

## E.SEC12 — the audit is blind to the lane it exists to police, and the live compose step rides it

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| PREREGISTERED BEFORE CODE | Yes. |
| THE FINDING, and it qualifies E.SEC9's headline | The guard matches `.chat(` and deliberately excludes `chat_grounded(`/`chat_scoped(` — "the character after `chat` is `_`, not `(`". Excluding `chat_grounded` is right: that IS the private lane. Excluding `chat_scoped` is not, because **`chat_scoped(…, PrivacyScope::Household)` is the Household lane spelled differently** — the exact risk class the guard was built to find. Twelve such call sites exist and the audit has never seen one of them. |
| E.SEC9 said "UNSCOPED_PENDING is empty". That was true and misleading | It audited `chat()` and was structurally incapable of auditing `chat_scoped(Household)`. Closing every door named `chat(` while a door named `chat_scoped(…, Household)` stood open is the ELEVENTH instance of tonight's one error: match one spelling of a thing, fix those, call it coverage. |
| WORSE — the live path is one of them | `agent_primary` defaults to TRUE and the box has no override, so every production turn goes through the agent loop. The plain composition path I spent E.SEC8 slice 4 and E.SEC9 grounding is the LEGACY chain, `with_agent_primary(false)`, used by tests. `build_prompt` is reachable only from it. So the transcript/mail/GitHub channel gating I built in the "fourth pass" does not execute in production at all. |
| The live compose step carries everything and declares Household | After the loop, `chat_streamed_to_progress` writes the final answer from the work log AND the grounding — every recalled belief the turn gathered. Both its branches are Household: `chat_streaming_sink` gates on `PrivacyScope::Household`, and the fallback says so explicitly. The code KNOWS: "the privacy audit is right that compose carries household memory… Moving compose to the private-grounded lane is a deliberate future sweep." E.SEC9 was meant to BE that sweep and could not see it. |
| What is and is not still true of E.SEC8/E.SEC9 | Still true, and probe-verified on the live path: the output-scope gate fires in `turn_grounding`, the agent loop's transcript is withheld under total prohibition, and the loop gets no tools. The live disclosure failure does not reproduce. NOT true as stated: "the main conversational turn is grounded" — that was the dormant path. The live turn's steps use `chat_grounded_tools` (private), but its COMPOSE rides Household. |
| PREDICTION | Widening the guard to treat `chat_scoped(…, Household)` as unscoped will flag around a dozen sites, most of them in `emissary.rs`, plus the compose pair. Public-scoped calls should NOT flag: `PrivacyScope::Public` is a declaration that the content is public, which is a different claim from Household. |
| KILL criteria | (1) The widened guard must FIRE on the compose call — verified by watching it fail, not by reading it. (2) It must NOT fire on `chat_grounded`, or it has stopped distinguishing the private lane. (3) It must not fire on `chat_scoped(…, Public)`. (4) Every newly-flagged site gets a decision recorded — grounded, or allowlisted with a reason — not a blanket deferral. (5) Workspace green. |
| NOT claimed | That compose should move to the private lane today. That is the decision the code already deferred once, it is on the live path serving every turn, and `chat_grounded` fails closed — so moving it trades a conditional cloud failover for a hard outage whenever the owned cluster is down. Pranab has already ruled once on exactly that trade for the main turn; this is the same question for compose, and it is his and Codex's, not mine. |
